  Phrasal verbs are easy to understand. Everything you need is to know meanings of the prepositions what placed after main verbs. For example, the very phrasal verb "keep up" - keep is to hold sth or continue to do sth and preposition "up" is as teacher said is to increase/improve sth or it's some movement or direction - I climb up the mountain or I walk down the stairs. In this case the preposition UP points to direction of movement. Now let's return to the word keep up - Keep it up means Continue improving it. As you can see, I simply put together 2 meanings and got the new word. That way you can learn a lot of different phrasal verbs. If you know meanings of the preposition what's used in your phrasal verb, you'll be able to learn a lot of phrasal verbs

**@Suliztin** (👍1 · 2012-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4wpjb5rvFZA&lc=UgwVZfe3iyiQyJfherZ4AaABAg
  You are correct, but not by intuition.  'to stay up' can only be used as an active verb, meaning the subject does the action of 'not going to bed',  'to be kept up' is used in the passive here, meaning sb else does the action, which affects the subject.  Used in the active form, it means that you do sthg to stop sb from going to bed.  It's grammar, not just 'what sounds right'

  "I was kept up by the loud music".

  This guy is a good teacher, with a good teaching style. 
  
  But in England, 'muggy' simply means humid.
  
  Also, in England (and the rest of the UK) we call the fall 'Autumn'. 
  
  IMHO 'Autumn' is the better name for the season - because it cannot mean anything else but Autumn (it has no other definition)... Just as 'Summer' only means summer and 'Winter' only means Winter.
  
  'Fall' is what you do when you jump off a cliff. lool :-)

**@deliapauline123** (👍28 · 2014-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7ZWXtyavnMw&lc=UghGygpzAZqLy3gCoAEC
  I agree that the emphasis with the present perfect simple is on the result rather than the action but is far more than a reference to quantity or numbers. For example, 'You've had your hair cut!' emphasises the result (a new look!) or I've lost my keys! (I can't get into my house) but these have nothing to do with numbers!
  It's important not to let a rule of thumb become an overgeneralisaiton or students will apply it indescriminately and this will confuse them more than anything else.
  Other examples of present perfect sentences that emphasise result but have nothing to do with numbers: 'I've let the dog out. ' 'I've had enough!' 
  Anyway, I like your videos and think they are clear and captivating

  James you're a perfect example of why a being a native speaker is not a sufficient condition to be a good English teacher. 
  
  Present perfect simple = The emphasis is on a time span we can resume with... (____your life up to now). EX I've never lived in Italy, I've always enjoyed cinema, Have you ever tried bunjee jumping?
  
  ALSO, we can use this tense with FOR and SINCE to talk about situations that began in the past and continue in the present.
  
  EX. I've lived here for ten years, I haven't seen her since last summer... etc.
  
  Buy a damn grammar book, son. Murphy should do.

**@DeersThrone** (👍9 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nWqMQ26Gqi4&lc=UghD9KXJ4KqNnXgCoAEC
  In high school, I was told by one or two teachers to NOT ask a question. However, I think in college things like that are a little less strict...
    ↳ **@shaneduckworth7288** (👍8 · 2015-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nWqMQ26Gqi4&lc=UghD9KXJ4KqNnXgCoAEC.7-H0Z7-J45c7-sL5crYjdM
      I think you're a bit confused about the question thing. What teachers mean when they say that is that your thesis should not BE a question. Your thesis should be the answer to the question and your essay will be about exploring and proving that answer. For instance, if you're asked to write a character analysis from the Harry Potter series, you start by asking questions: "Why did J. K. Rowling make up a character like...Nevel or Dobie or Snape?" "What role does that character play--physically, emotionally, philosophically?" For instance, if I picked Snape I could ask questions about who he really is (as opposed to how he showed himself throughout the books) and what lessons Rowling was maybe trying to teach us through this character. I might even be able to draw from real live people who chose to hide their true selves for the greater good (watch Valkyrie w/ Tom Cruise). My thesis would grow out from my answers to these questions. The real first step of an essay is to ask questions then RESEARCH for the answer. It's not to come up with your thesis and then "prove" it. This leads to looking for information that supports your thesis rather than allowing your thesis to develop from your understanding of a topic. Hope that clears things up for you.

**@XXXXcloudangellXXXX** (👍4 · 2016-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=nWqMQ26Gqi4&lc=Ugg20CEALphG_ngCoAEC
  If essay is about internet: good or bad? [Easiest example]
  1st para:
  Intro
  Thesis statement
  Rhetorical question
  1 Advantage and 1 Disadvantage. 
  At least 5 sentences
  
  2nd para: 
  This paragraph can be an advantage or disadvantage. Take all the points in mind and connect them by linking words, very important. 
  
  3rd para:
  If it's a disadvantage, first line must be "On the contrary", then start with points followed by linking words. 
  
  4th para:
  The first line must be telling about the author's opinion. Conclude the whole essay by summarizing the whole thing in a sentence or two. 
  
  Well, this is what I learned at school, care to share yours?

      My edited version to Sample #1: John & Mary was absent from school today. John is a good friend, who always inquire for help; I am sometimes happy to give my friend assistance when needed. However, Mary usually tells me, that he does not need my help and that he should learn to do his own work😩.
      
      Sample #2: the mistakes (“your” *you are*, “Its” *It has*, “don’t” *do not*, “you’re” *your*). 
      My edited version: Please to let me know when you are ready for us to talk. I think we have a lot to touch base on or to talk about, for it has been a difficult time for the both of us. And to be honest, I don’t know if I am able to forgive you for the poor actions you have made.
      
      Sample #3: I did not check the weather forecast. Gladly, a friend of mine had told me that it was going to rain tomorrow. However, I made sure to bring an umbrella ☔️ when our group of friends, travelled out to the Yosemite National Park.
      
      The corrections are: “check weather” checked the weather*, “a friend told me” *a friend told me, , “a umbrella” *an umbrella*.

**@Anna-mc3ll** (👍15 · 2025-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gFXE9n7hrOI&lc=Ugy6Fb17er13uCj8_ul4AaABAg
  Descriptive writing:
  
  to plead - to make an urgent emotional statement or request for something
  
  to beg - to make a very strong and urgent request
  
  to entreat - to try very hard to persuade someone to do something
  
  to implore - to ask someone to do or not do something in a very sincere, emotional, and determined way
  
  to beseech - to ask for something in a way that shows you need it very much
  
  to cajole - to persuade someone to do something they might not want to do, by pleasant talk and promises
  
  to insist - to say firmly or demand forcefully, especially when others disagree or oppose what you say
  
  to demand - to ask for something forcefully, in a way that shows that you do not expect to be refused
  
  to gush - to express a positive feeling, especially praise, sometimes in such a strong way that it does not sound sincere
  
  to cheer - to give a loud shout of approval or encouragement
  
  to tout - to advertise, talk about, or praise something or someone repeatedly, especially as a way of encouraging people to like, accept, or buy something
  
  to laugh - to smile while making sounds with your voice that show you think something is funny or you are happy
  
  to chortle - to laugh, showing pleasure and satisfaction
  
  to giggle - to laugh repeatedly in a quiet but uncontrolled way, often at something silly or rude or when you are nervous
  
  to proclaim - to announce something publicly or officially, especially something positive
  
  to announce - to make something known or tell people about something officially
  
  to declare - to announce something clearly, firmly, publicly, or officially 
  
  to pout - to show annoyance, especially by pressing the lips together or pushing out the lower lip
  
  to complain - to say that something is wrong or not satisfactory
  
  to sob - to cry noisily, taking in deep breaths
  
  to cry - to produce tears as the result of a strong emotion, such as unhappiness or pain
  
  to lament - to express sadness and feeling sorry about something
  
  to sneer - to talk about someone or something in an unkind way that shows no respect
  
  to whine - to make a long, high, sad sound
  
  to bemoan - to complain or express sadness about something
  
  to shout - to speak with a very loud voice, often as loud as possible, usually when you want to make yourself heard in noisy situations
  
  to exclaim - to say or shout something suddenly because of surprise, fear, pleasure, etc.
  
  to bellow - to shout in a loud voice
  
  to murmur - to speak or say something very quietly
  
  to mutter - to speak quietly and in a low voice that is not easy to hear
  
  to mumble - to speak quietly and in a way that is not clear, so that the words are difficult to understand
  
  to whisper - to speak very quietly, so that only the person close to you can hear you

**@Mrradio01-15** (👍0 · 2026-07-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gFXE9n7hrOI&lc=UgzSZQS231yiInKZGtZ4AaABAg
  Very informative video! I haven’t written anything very much until a few years ago (2022) since high school and I am now 31. I enjoyed writing a lot in school thanks to a couple of very good teachers in elementary school and I thought I was ok at it. This video has given me much better skills! Now bear with me when I say this very crazy thing: Use a typewriter for your first drafts. I kid you not when I say that I find it very helpful for concentration and getting those initial thoughts onto paper. No distractions from the computer: It’s just you and the machine. No annoying notifications from emails, social media  or spell check  or other things like that I find very distracting. It will bug me until I go and make the corrections Microsoft Word  tells me to. Meanwhile, I have lost my train of thought of the initial purpose I was writing that sentence for. Trust me when I say that  first draft is going to be VERY ugly (grammar and spelling wise) if you’re not used to being without a computer like I was when you proofread it. HOWEVER, the thoughts will be completely yours. Typewriters are after all completely dependent  on you. It only puts onto paper what you tell it to. It makes you think about what you are writing, unless you like using white out or starting over 😂. The return is it will not judge your writing and it aids in the creative process. It’s all about getting those thoughts onto paper. I then take what I have done on the typewriter, proofread it as many times as I see fit, make corrections based on what I see and then fine tune it on the computer. I am still living in the 21st century too. As far as what typewriter to get, that is a different rabbit hole entirely but there are great people on YouTube who will help you out with that. I went from not knowing much about typewriters and a little curiosity to getting it and typing every word that I know like Ron Swanson from Parks and Recreation at my grandma’s house where I got my typewriter. Now I have my own method of writing that is fun and apparently there are communities of other people who think like I do! Happy writing and enjoy yourselves!

  Hi, Lisa. Tks 4 yr adorable videos. Whenever I feel like learning new idioms and phrasal verbs, I turn to you. In the nutshell, I have been writing your precious explanation, so that I have them (idioms and phrasal verbs) in flash cards for me to see them every now and then,  and I try to use them in contexts as I write to friends who happen to teach or enjoy English. Back in the 80s I took the TOEFL in California and have shared it with my pupils who were to take the test. In order to garner more knowledge , I have memorized all idioms in context by Helen K. Fragiadakis´ All Clear. Now, with the "inernet" as Americans say it, we are lucky to have awesome teachers like you to guide and lead us. A special Thank-you from Brazil. Keep up the good work, please!

  She is an excellent English  language teacher. In a world where globalised business, etc, will be the norm, it will become critical to not only have basic command of English, the current international 'lingua franca' that has replaced the former French, but to also be familiar with its many idiomatic expressions and phrsal verbs that baffle many otherwise fairly fluent speakers of English as a second language or native speakers   of other English dialects, like Americans. Welcome to Australian idiomatic English usage we assume everybody understands. We have so many such uniquely Australian expression they fill an entire dictionary. We are not even aware of the effect this has on other speakers and wonder why they 'just don't twig onto' what we are tring to say. We conclude they must be 'pretty dense'. And, if they still don't 'get us', and 'what we're on about', we have some stronger Australian language to describe such people who 'just don't get it', like 'dim wit', 'block head', 'got nothing between their ears', plus a few more idiomatic expressions too graphic and obscene to mention here, typically used in advanced, by now, ANGRY, stages of defective communication.

  Today we have a lot on our plate: our sales fell, lost market share and laid off many of our workers.
  
  To address the problem we have to think outside the box and put ourselves on the cutting edge. But without having the ballpark figure of the initial cost we can’t get the ball rolling on our restructuring . The bottom line is to carry out an assessment in order not to put all our eggs in basket. 
  This is the only way that we can put all the team members on the same page. 
  Through accurate information every one of us will go to the extra mile if the data analysts cross the t’s and dot the i’s during the data analysis and presentation. The employees are to hit the nail on the head and should not keep the elephant in the room.
  
  The CEO should also cover all the bases to play catch-up the market trends not to play it by ear. 
  Putting out the fires and getting the green light from the board of directors is crucial too. Otherwise everything will be up in the air.Therefore,everyone has to avoid going down in the hole of the rabbit and pick the low-hanging fruit. 
  Finally anyone who drops the ball on the deadline of their tasks will get the axe.

  What I observe and find interesting about English idiomatic business English usage is the number of idioms that involve reference to competitiveness and sports eg 'ball', 'ball park', 'catch up'. And ones that are downright violent, aggressive eg 'axe', 'hammer', 'hit', 'trigger'. And if they are not violent, aggressive, competitive, they make reference to things like power, size eg 'elephant'. The psychology of language is fascinating and can tell ys much about the speaker that thry are not explictly revealing about thrmselves, but a perceptive linguist will quickly pick up and use this to assess the personality type they are interacting with.

  Dear teacher! Is it you who kill my time-code posts instead of saying thank you or is it some other malady? Here I post it for the third time.
  0:40; 4:00 a ballpark figure
  0:55; 4:40 get the ball rolling
  1:15 to give the green light
  2:00 to think outside the box
  3:00 cutting-edge
  5:20 the bottom line
  5:50 to put all your eggs in one busket
  6:30 to be on the same page
  7:20 to go the extra mile
  7:50 to cross the t's and dot the i's
  8:50 the elefant in the room
  9:50 to hit the nail on the head
  10:25 to cover all the bases
  10:55 to play it by ear
  11:40 to play catch-up
  12:30 to put out fires
  15:15 to get the green light
  16:10 up in the air

  I used "think outside the box"  in one of my graduate research papers and my professor asked me what I had meant. Idioms are most useful in casual conversations and not in formal writing, such as a research paper.  At least that was my take away.
    ↳ **@Blacktsalagi73** (👍1 · 2024-08-12) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JqJGKt_D1no&lc=UgwfTVHA-JcqfEn8Wbh4AaABAg.A1IF73vMKzSA2N3Ubiev_S
      That depends on what your professor actually meant. 
      Did they mean: What "you" meant by the expression or what "the expression" (itself) meant? 
      One of the differences when writing would be to reinforce the expression with a brief contextual phrase or clause. 
      If not, then perhaps a research paper might not be a good idea when writing. 
      In the business world, whether or not you use them, not understanding them can be a challenge regarding comprehension.
